SGD with Dependent Data: Optimal Estimation, Regret, and Inference
Yinan Shen, Yichen Zhang, Wen-Xin Zhou

Abstract
This work investigates the performance of the final iterate produced by stochastic gradient descent (SGD) under temporally dependent data. We consider two complementary sources of dependence: ( i ) (i) martingale-type dependence in both the covariate and noise processes, which accommodates non-stationary and non-mixing time series data, and ( i ​ i ) (ii) dependence induced by sequential decision making. Our formulation runs in parallel with classical notions of (local) stationarity and strong mixing, while neither framework fully subsumes the other. Remarkably, SGD is shown to automatically accommodate both independent and dependent information under a broad class of stepsize schedules and exploration rate schemes. Non-asymptotically, we show that SGD simultaneously achieves statistically optimal estimation error and regret, extending and improving existing results. In particular, our tail bounds remain sharp even for potentially infinite horizon T = + ∞ T=+\infty . Asymptotically, the SGD iterates converge to a Gaussian distribution with only an O ℙ ​ ( 1 / t ) O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1/\sqrt{t}) remainder, demonstrating that the supposed estimation-regret trade-off claimed in prior work can in fact be avoided. We further propose a new “conic” approximation of the decision region that allows the covariates to have unbounded support. For online sparse regression, we develop a new SGD-based algorithm that uses only d d units of storage and requires O ⁡ ( d ) O(d) flops per iteration, achieving the long term statistical optimality. Intuitively, each incoming observation contributes to estimation accuracy, while aggregated summary statistics guide support recovery.



Keywords
: asymptotic distribution, conditional Orlicz norm, decision making, martingale, non-asymptotic analysis, stochastic gradient descent, sparse regression

1
Introduction

Stochastic gradient descent (SGD) is a fundamental algorithm for online learning and sequential decision making. Despite its simplicity, understanding its statistical behavior becomes considerably more challenging when the incoming data exhibit dependence, either because the covariates or noise form a time series, or because the data are collected adaptively through the learner’s decision. Such dependence is pervasive in modern applications, including contextual bandits, financial and economic forecasting
(
7
;
61
;
34
)
, and streaming prediction problems
(
53
;
41
;
66
;
68
;
67
)
. Consequently, a central methodological and theoretical question is how to design and analyze SGD procedures that automatically adapt to potentially mixed sources of independent and dependent observations, without requiring explicit knowledge of their underlying dependence structure.

A substantial literature has developed tools for analyzing dependent processes, spanning (local) stationarity and physical or functional dependence measures
(
18
;
68
;
69
;
49
;
58
)
,
\rho
-mixing and other related weak dependence conditions
(
47
;
20
;
48
;
45
)
, and density-based characterizations of conditional distributions
(
60
)
. While these frameworks provide powerful theoretical guarantees, they typically rely on precise characterizations of temporal dependence, such as stationarity, mixing rates, or conditional density. However, modern online environments frequently violate these assumptions: covariates may be neither stationary nor mixing, noise distributions may evolve over time, and adaptive data collection introduces additional dependence channels that are not adequately captured by classical stochastic process theory
(
2
;
39
;
62
;
42
)
.

This paper develops an estimation and inference framework for SGD that accommodates the simultaneous presence of independent and dependent observations, without attempting to classify or disentangle their sources. Our formulation parallels or extends classical dependence concepts but is more flexible. In particular, covariates may evolve according to processes that are neither independent across time nor locally stationary nor strongly mixing, yet still fall within the scope of our theory. Moreover, adaptively collected rewards in contextual bandit settings introduce a second source of dependence: the evolving decision rule interacts with the data-generating mechanism, jointly shaping the observed sequence. Our framework incorporates both types of dependence within a unified analysis. To illustrate the wide range of dependence structures encompassed by our analysis, consider a sequence
d
-dimensional covariates
\{{\mathbf{X}}_{t}\}
evolving according to either of the following dynamics: (i)
{\mathbf{X}}_{t}=\nu_{t}{\mathbf{X}}_{t-1}/\|{\mathbf{X}}_{t-1}\|+{\mathbf{E}}_{t}
, or (ii)
{\mathbf{X}}_{0}\sim\mathrm{Unif}({\mathbb{S}}^{d-1})
and
{\mathbf{X}}_{t}=\nu_{t,0}{\mathbf{X}}_{0}+\cdots+\nu_{t,t-1}{\mathbf{X}}_{t-1}+{\mathbf{E}}_{t}
for
t\geq 1
, where
\{\nu_{t}\}
,
\{\nu_{t,i}\}
and
\{{\mathbf{E}}_{t}\}
are random but not necessarily stationary or independent, with
\sum_{i=0}^{t}|\nu_{t,i}|\leq 1/2
. In such settings, classical stationary or mixing analyses are generally inapplicable. Nevertheless, we show that under suitable regularity conditions on the covariates and noise, the non-asymptotic performance of SGD with these dependent covariates can be established at a level comparable to the idealized i.i.d. Gaussian setting. Furthermore, under mild convergence and finite-moment conditions, we derive the asymptotic distribution of the SGD iterates.

The same type of dependence may also arise in the noise process, which is likewise accommodated by our framework. More broadly, the dependence considered in this work is two-fold: in addition to the intrinsic temporal dependence of covariates and noise, we incorporate the dependence induced by decision making. This latter form of dependence is intrinsic to contextual bandit problems
(
29
;
33
;
5
;
4
;
15
;
12
;
13
;
31
;
21
;
30
)
, where the learner observes covariates, selects an action, and receives a reward accordingly. The balance between exploration (random pulls) and exploitation (pulling the empirically best arm) is crucial. Existing exploration strategies fall into two categories. The first relies on a prespecified grid of exploration times
(
29
;
33
;
5
;
4
;
52
)
, which enables elegant offline analysis but requires storing and repeatedly using historical observations, as well as prior knowledge of the total horizon. The second category consists of
\varepsilon
-greedy-type schemes
(
15
;
13
;
12
;
31
;
14
;
21
)
, which specify the exploration probability
\pi_{t}
without knowledge of the horizon and do not store the full data stream. While this leads to practically appealing fully online algorithms, it also introduces substantial theoretical challenges. In particular, existing online analyses under independent data typically require relatively high exploration rates, often assuming either
\lim_{t\to\infty}\pi_{t}>0
or
\pi_{t}=t^{-\alpha}
with
\alpha\in(0,1)
, resulting in regret rates of order
O(T)
or
O(T^{2/3})
, which are strictly worse than offline guarantees.

This work aims to close this gap. We allow a broad class of exploration schedules, including choices of the form
\pi_{t}=f(t)
for functions
f
specified in Definition
3
. Despite this flexibility, our algorithm attains simultaneously optimal estimation error and regret. To incorporate dependent streaming data without storing past observations, we update parameters via SGD and introduce a new family of stepsize schedules. As shown in Section
4
, when
f(t)
satisfies Definition
3
and the growth condition
\int_{0}^{T}f(t){\rm d}t\leq O(\sqrt{T})
, both the regret and estimation error achieve minimax-optimal rates, even for discrete covariates. Although the covariates and noise may be dependent, our non-asymptotic guarantees match the optimal offline rates that assume i.i.d. data.

Another challenge in fully online analysis, without retaining historical data, is the accumulation of tail probabilities
(
35
;
15
;
13
;
43
;
21
;
57
)
. This issue forces prior work to restrict the horizon to
T=O(d^{C})
or
T\leq\exp(cd)
. Our analysis avoids this limitation: under our SGD algorithm, all non-asymptotic guarantees remain valid for
T=+\infty
. In Section
2
, by leveraging the lower bound results of
46
, we further show that our result is statistically optimal among algorithms that allow
t=1,\ldots,+\infty
. Asymptotically, we obtain a Bahadur-type representation

\sqrt{t}\left({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\right)=\text{a term that converges to Gaussian in distribution}+O_{{\mathbb{P}}}\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\bigg),

which is sharp for an online procedure. Existing online methods, even under i.i.d. data and with weaker regret guarantees, incur residuals of order
t^{1/5}
due to dependence effects. The multifold optimality achieved here stems from the coordinated design of both the algorithms and the analysis.

Online sparse linear regression has long remained a challenging problem. How to estimate a sparse parameter in an online manner is largely open. Conceptually, online sparse linear regression integrates estimation in Euclidean norm
(
8
;
9
;
6
;
59
)
with support recovery
(
26
;
11
;
24
;
72
)
. As streaming data arrive, the goal is to achieve a monotonically decreasing estimation error while eventually recovering the entire support. However, existing offline theories and assumptions do not directly transfer to the online setting. For example, offline analyses often require a lower bound on the minimal signal strength
(
72
)
, such as
\min_{j:\beta_{j}\neq 0}|\beta_{j}|\geq O(1/\sqrt{n})
for a fixed sample size
n
. In contrast, online learning may involve an unknown or unbounded horizon. To circumvent this difficulty,
25
assume that the initialization step already identifies the true support, though they still require a minimal signal strength condition.
32
and
70
develop online LASSO algorithms, but they recompute a full LASSO estimator each time a new observation arrives. All three studies focus on sparse estimation under i.i.d. observations.

The algorithm of SGD, or stochastic approximation, dates back to
53
;
38
;
51
. Although the final iterate of SGD is widely used in practice, its statistical performance, especially whether it can match that of offline estimators in terms of estimation error and non-asymptotic guarantees, remains largely unclear. This work investigates the behavior of the last iterate of SGD without storing historical data and without averaging over past iterates. Below, we summarize our main contributions and Section
5
numerically verifies our theoretical results.

1.

Dependent and Independent Data:
Our dependence structure runs in parallel to classical stationary or mixing frameworks, and neither class contains the other. Specifically, we generalize the i.i.d. Gaussian setting to random variables with bounded conditional Orlicz norms. Interestingly, even when the covariates and noise exhibit dependence, the non-asymptotic performance of SGD remains essentially the same as in the i.i.d. Gaussian case. Asymptotic results are established under mild convergence and moment conditions, and the Markov structure does not degrade the asymptotic performance of SGD. The resulting Bahadur-type remainder is as small as
1/\sqrt{t}
.

2.

Performance of SGD in Linear Regression:
Whether online SGD can yield estimators that are statistically comparable to offline approaches has remained unknown even for linear regression. In Section
2
, we investigate its non-asymptotic and asymptotic performance in the presence of potentially dependent covariates and noise, without involving decision making. From a non-asymptotic perspective, we show that the tail probability

\max_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}(\{{\mathbf{X}}_{l},Y_{l}\}_{l=1}^{t})}{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\cup_{t=0}^{T}\bigg\{\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|^{2}\leq\frac{Cd}{t}\bigg\}\bigg)

is at least
1-T\exp(-cd)
, matching the best offline results. Moreover, we show that the trajectory

\cup_{t=0}^{+\infty}\bigg\{\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|^{2}\leq C\frac{d+\log t}{t}\bigg\}

holds with probability at least
1-\exp(-cd)
. Borrowing results from
46
, we further illustrate that our estimation error and tail probability are optimal, up to constants. Existing studies focus on the i.i.d. observations and require
T\leq d^{C}
or
T\leq\exp(cd)

(
35
;
15
;
13
;
43
;
21
;
57
)
.

3.

Decision Making and Linear Bandit:
Whether SGD can simultaneously achieve statistical optimality in estimation and regret in an online manner has been unknown, even under i.i.d. covariates and noise. This work closes this gap and establishes such results under dependent covariates and noise. Our guarantees hold under minimal assumptions for a broad class of exploration rates and stepsizes, including rates generated by general functions in Definition
3
. We further show that the SGD iterates under our stepsize scheme converge to a multivariate Gaussian. In addition, we establish the first inference results for both decaying and zero exploration rates by fully incorporating dependent data. The proposed stepsize scheme is also new.

4.

Online Sparse Learning:
Designing an online algorithm for sparse linear regression that neither stores the full data nor solves a new optimization problem whenever a new sample arrives has remained open. This work advances the state of the art by proposing a new SGD-based algorithm for sparse linear regression with storage cost
d
and per-iteration computation
O(d)
. Intuitively, each individual observation contributes to improving estimation accuracy, while summary statistics are used for variable selection. Our algorithm achieves statistically optimal iterates in the long run.

5.

Conic Decision Region:
This work introduces a new conic-shaped approximation of the true decision making region, which allows the covariates to have possibly unbounded support and to substantially cover a neighborhood around the origin. Additionally, our asymptotic results are based on the conic measurement, which removes the continuous conditions of the distribution in existing works
(
14
;
15
;
13
)
. When covariates are discretely distributed, our characterization coincides with those used in
5
and
4
.

In addition, the statistical theory developed in this work may be of independent interest. We conclude this section by introducing the notation used throughout the paper.

Notation

We use bold symbols to denote vectors and matrices (e.g.,
{\mathbf{g}},{\mathbf{X}},{\mathbf{Y}}
), and calligraphic font to denote sets, operators, or
\sigma
-algebras (e.g.,
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{F}}
). Throughout,
[K]
denotes the set
\{1,2,\ldots,K\}
. For two symmetric matrices
{\mathbf{A}}
and
{\mathbf{B}}
,
{\mathbf{A}}\succeq{\mathbf{B}}
means that
{\mathbf{A}}-{\mathbf{B}}
has non-negative eigenvalues. The operator
\|\cdot\|
denotes the
L_{2}
norm for vectors and the operator norm for matrices, while
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
denotes the maximum absolute entry of a vector. For a symmetric matrix
{\mathbf{A}}={\mathbf{U}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d}
with
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{d})
and a function
f(\cdot)
, we define
f({\mathbf{A}})={\mathbf{U}}f(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}){\mathbf{U}}^{\top}
and
f(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})=\operatorname{diag}(f(\lambda_{1}),\ldots,f(\lambda_{d}))
. We write
\rightsquigarrow
for convergence in distribution.

For a vector
{\mathbf{X}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,
[{\mathbf{X}}]_{i}
denotes its
i
-th entry, and for a set
{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq[d]
,
[{\mathbf{X}}]_{{\mathcal{S}}}
denotes the subvector of
{\mathbf{X}}
with indices in
{\mathcal{S}}
. In the context of sparse linear regression, the operator
{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{S}}}(\cdot):{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
retains only the entries in
{\mathcal{S}}
and sets the entries in
{\mathcal{S}}^{c}
to zero; that is,
[{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{S}}}({\mathbf{X}})]_{i}=[{\mathbf{X}}]_{i}
for
i\in{\mathcal{S}}
and
0
for
i\in{\mathcal{S}}^{c}
.

2
Linear Regression with Dependent Data

We begin by introducing the conditional Orlicz norm, which plays a central role in our analysis. This concept is not new; rather, it generalizes several cases. Foundational work such as
39
,
63
,
62
,
55
, and
42
, along with the classical Azuma’s inequality
(
2
)
, has explored concentration inequalities for dependent random variables that are either bounded or satisfy bounded conditional sub-Gaussian tails. More recently, as non-asymptotic bounds for the estimation error of stochastic gradient descent (SGD) have attracted increasing attention, conditional Orlicz norm-based techniques have been employed in works such as
31
and
57
. However, these studies focus on independent covariates and noise. Our work substantially generalizes the framework by allowing for more general dependence structures. In particular, we show that under suitable conditions, SGD under dependent data performs comparably to the idealized i.i.d. Gaussian setting.

2.1
Preliminaries: Orlicz norm and Conditional Orlicz Norm

Recall that the Orlicz norm of a univariate random variable is defined as

\|X\|_{\Psi_{\alpha}}:=\inf\big\{u>0:\,{\mathbb{E}}\exp(|X/u|^{\alpha})\leq 2\big\},\quad\alpha\geq 1.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a
\sigma
-field. A random variable
K_{{\mathcal{F}}}
is said to be
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable if
K_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}
.

Definition 1

(Conditional Orlicz Norm)
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{F}}
is a
\sigma
-field and
K_{{\mathcal{F}}}>0
is
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable. A random variable
X
satisfies
\|X|{\mathcal{F}}\|_{\Psi_{\alpha}}\leq K_{{\mathcal{F}}}
if and only if

{\mathbb{E}}\big\{\exp(|X/K_{{\mathcal{F}}}|^{\alpha})|{\mathcal{F}}\big\}\leq 2\quad a.s.

(1)

Note that the conditional expectation above is itself
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable random variable. We illustrate the concept of the conditional Orlicz norm with the following examples.

Example 1
.

If
X
is
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable, then
\|X|{\mathcal{F}}\|_{\Psi_{\alpha}}\leq(\log 2)^{-1/\alpha}\cdot|X|
. If
X
is independent of
{\mathcal{F}}
, then
\|X|{\mathcal{F}}\|_{\Psi_{\alpha}}\leq\|X\|_{\Psi_{2}}
.

For a random vector
{\mathbf{X}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, define

\|{\mathbf{X}}\|_{\Psi_{\alpha}}:=\sup_{{\mathbf{u}}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}}\|{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}{\mathbf{X}}\|_{\Psi_{\alpha}}.

The conditional version is defined analogously as follows.

Definition 2

(Conditional Orlicz Norm of Random Vectors)
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{F}}
is a
\sigma
-field and
K_{{\mathcal{F}}}>0
is
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable. A random vector
{\mathbf{X}}
satisfies
\|{\mathbf{X}}|{\mathcal{F}}\|_{\Psi_{\alpha}}\leq K_{{\mathcal{F}}}
if and only if

\sup_{{\mathbf{u}}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1},{\mathbf{u}}\in{\mathcal{F}}}{\mathbb{E}}\big\{\exp(|{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}{\mathbf{X}}/K_{{\mathcal{F}}}|^{\alpha})|{\mathcal{F}}\big\}\leq 2\quad\text{a.s.}

(2)

Additionally, for a random matrix
{\mathbf{X}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}
, we define

\|{\mathbf{X}}\|_{\Psi_{\alpha}}:=\sup_{{\mathbf{u}}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d_{1}-1}}\sup_{{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d_{2}-1}}\|{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}{\mathbf{X}}{\mathbf{v}}\|_{\Psi_{\alpha}},

with conditional norms defined similarly. These norms are well defined and satisfy the usual norm properties; details are deferred to Appendix
A
. Finally, we present another illustrative example of the conditional Orlicz norm, which can be verified directly.

Example 2
.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}=\sigma(X)
. Define
Y_{1}=X+\xi
and
Y_{2}=\xi X
, where
\xi
is independent of
X
and
\|\xi\|_{\Psi_{\alpha}}<\infty
. Then

\|Y_{1}|{\mathcal{F}}\|_{\Psi_{\alpha}}\leq|X|/\log 2+\|\xi\|_{\Psi_{\alpha}},\quad\|Y_{2}|{\mathcal{F}}\|_{\Psi_{\alpha}}\leq|X|\cdot\|\xi\|_{\Psi_{\alpha}}.

If
|X|\leq K
almost surely,
\|Y_{1}|{\mathcal{F}}\|_{\Psi_{\alpha}}\leq K/\log 2+\|\xi\|_{\Psi_{\alpha}}
and
\|Y_{2}|{\mathcal{F}}\|_{\Psi_{\alpha}}\leq K\cdot\|\xi\|_{\Psi_{\alpha}}
.

2.2
Linear Regression with Dependent Covariates and Noise

To isolate the effects of dependence from those arising from decision making, we first study dependent-data linear regression via SGD. At each time
t
, we observe
({\mathbf{X}}_{t},Y_{t})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}
generated from the linear model

\displaystyle Y_{t}={\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}+\xi_{t},

where
{\mathbf{X}}_{t}
and
\xi_{t}
may depend on past observations. The goal is to estimate
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
in an online manner, processing each data point as it arrives without storing the entire history. We allow an arbitrary initialization
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. At time
t
, suppose the current iterate is
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t-1}
and a new data pair
({\mathbf{X}}_{t},Y_{t})
becomes available. We then update the parameter using the standard SGD rule:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t+1}={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}-\eta_{t}\cdot({\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}-Y_{t}){\mathbf{X}}_{t},

(3)

where
\eta_{t}
is the stepsize to be specified later. Let
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}=\sigma(Y_{t-1},{\mathbf{X}}_{t-1},\ldots,Y_{0},{\mathbf{X}}_{0})
denote the
\sigma
-field generated by all randomness up to time
t-1
, and define
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{+}=\sigma({\mathbf{X}}_{t},Y_{t-1},{\mathbf{X}}_{t-1},\ldots,Y_{0},{\mathbf{X}}_{0})
. The update in (
3
) ensures that
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}
is
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
-measurable, and that
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{+}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}_{t+1}
. We next establish the convergence of (
3
) under the following assumptions.

Assumption 1

(Covariates: Martingale Difference with Conditional Sub-Gaussian Norm)
.

The covariate sequence
{\mathbf{X}}_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
satisfies
{\mathbb{E}}\{{\mathbf{X}}_{t}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}=0
a.s., and
\|{\mathbf{X}}_{t}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\|_{\Psi_{2}}^{2}\leq\lambda_{\max}
. Moreover, there exists
\lambda_{\min}>0
such that
{\mathbb{E}}\{{\mathbf{X}}_{t}{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}\succeq\lambda_{\min}\cdot{\mathbf{I}}_{d}
a.s. Here,
\lambda_{\min}
and
\lambda_{\max}
are constants.

Assumption 2

(Noise: Martingale Difference with Conditional Sub-Gaussian Norm)
.

The noise variable
\xi_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}
satisfies
{\mathbb{E}}\{\xi_{t}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{+}\}=0
a.s., and
\|\xi_{t}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{+}\|_{\Psi_{2}}\leq\sigma
, where
\sigma
is a constant.

Assumptions
1
and
2
allow both
{\mathbf{X}}_{t}
and
\xi_{t}
to depend on the historical data. This generalizes the commonly imposed assumptions of i.i.d. or uniformly bounded covariates, as seen in
50
,
27
,
15
,
31
,
56
,
43
,
1
,
65
,
32
and
57
. We emphasize that our framework is neither contained in nor contains the classical stationary or strong-mixing settings; the two regimes are fundamentally incomparable
(
68
;
69
)
.

Example 3
.

Let
{\mathbf{X}}_{0}
be any centered random vector satisfying
\|{\mathbf{X}}_{0}\|_{\Psi_{2}}<\infty
and
\lambda_{\min}({\mathbb{E}}\{{\mathbf{X}}_{0}{\mathbf{X}}_{0}^{\top}\})>0
. For
t\geq 1
, consider the process
{\mathbf{X}}_{t}=a_{t}\cdot\frac{{\mathbf{X}}_{t-1}}{\|{\mathbf{X}}_{t-1}\|}+{\mathbf{E}}_{t}
, where
{\mathbb{E}}\{a_{t}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}=0
,
|a_{t}|\leq 1
,
\|{\mathbf{E}}_{t}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\|_{\Psi_{2}}\leq\lambda
, and
{\mathbf{E}}_{t}\perp{\mathbf{X}}_{t-1}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
, with
\lambda_{\min}({\mathbb{E}}\{{\mathbf{E}}_{t}{\mathbf{E}}_{t}^{\top}\})>0
. In this construction,
\{{\mathbf{X}}_{t}\}
is dependent and may fail to be stationary or locally stationary. Nevertheless, it satisfies Assumptions
1
and
2
.

The next theorem establishes the non-asymptotic performance of SGD under two stepsize schemes. Interestingly, SGD can well incorporate the dependent data as if they were i.i.d. Gaussian, which is also verified with numeric experiments (Section
5
).

Theorem 1

(Non-asymptotic Performance)
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
and
2
hold. The iterates generated by update (
3
) satisfy:

1.

Constant Stepsize.
With a constant stepsize
\eta_{t}:=\eta\leq\lambda_{\min}/(d\lambda_{\max}^{2})
, the following holds with probability at least
1-(t+1)\exp(-c_{1}d)
:

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t+1}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|^{2}\leq 5(1-\eta\lambda_{\min}/2)^{t+1}\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|^{2}+5\frac{\lambda_{\max}}{\lambda_{\min}}\eta\sigma^{2}d.

In particular, after
t_{1}\geq\log(\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|/(\sqrt{\eta d}\sigma))
, we have
\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|^{2}\leq C\frac{\lambda_{\max}}{\lambda_{\min}}\eta\sigma^{2}d\leq C\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\lambda_{\max}}
.

2.

Decaying Stepsize.
Suppose some iteration
t_{1}
satisfies
\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|^{2}\leq\sigma^{2}/\lambda_{\max}
.
1
1

1

This condition is imposed for clarity of presentation. The proof shows a more general dynamic,

\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t+1}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|^{2}\leq C\left(\frac{d}{t+1+C_{b}d}\right)^{C_{a}-2}\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|^{2}+\frac{C}{\lambda_{\min}}\frac{\max\{d,\delta_{t+1}\}}{t+1+C_{b}d}\sigma^{2},

where the effect of the initial error shrinks rapidly when
C_{a}
is large. Since a constant stepsize yields linear decay in the initial error, we assume the simplified condition
\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|^{2}\leq\sigma^{2}/\lambda_{\max}
for exposition.
Take
\eta_{t}=\frac{C_{a}/\lambda_{\min}}{t-t_{1}+C_{b}d}
with
C_{a}\geq 2
and
C_{b}\geq 3C_{a}^{2}(\lambda_{\max}^{2}/\lambda_{\min}^{2})
. For any non-decreasing sequence
\{\delta_{t}\}
satisfying
(i)

0\leq\delta_{t}\leq\delta_{t+1}
,
(ii)

\delta_{t}\leq Ct/C_{b}
, we have, with probability at least
1-C\exp(-cd)-\sum_{l=t_{1}}^{t}\exp(-c(d+\delta_{l}))
, that for all
t\in[t_{1},+\infty]
,

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t+1}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|^{2}\leq C^{*}\ \frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}}\frac{\max\{d,\delta_{t+1}\}}{t+1+C_{b}d}\sigma^{2},\quad\text{where }C^{*}=2CC_{a}\frac{\lambda_{\max}}{\lambda_{\min}}.

The proof of part (1) is deferred to Appendix
A
. Part (2) is included as a special case of Theorem
4
, and thus its proof is omitted. Theorem
1
provides a two-phase online estimation strategy. The first phase, using a constant stepsize, ensures rapid linear contraction toward a neighborhood of
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}
; the radius of this neighborhood scales with the specified constant stepsize
\eta
. The second phase adopts a decaying stepsize and achieves statistically optimal performance, with error on the order of
O((d+\delta_{t})/t)
. Taking
\delta_{t}=c\log t
yields, with probability at least
1-C\exp(-cd)
, for all
t=t_{1},\ldots,+\infty
,

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|^{2}\leq C^{*}\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}}\frac{\max\{d,\log(t)\}}{t+C_{b}d}\sigma^{2}.

(4)

Compared with offline linear regression, the additional factor
\log(t)/t
in online learning is unavoidable to allow
t=+\infty
. Indeed,
46
shows that if
[{\mathbf{X}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{X}}_{t}]^{\top}
satisfies suitable conditions, then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\bigg\{\|\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{t}(\{{\mathbf{X}}_{l},Y_{l}\}_{l=1}^{t})-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|\geq c\sigma^{2}\cdot\frac{d+\log(\delta)}{t}\bigg\}\geq\frac{1}{\delta}.

Setting
\delta=t
gives

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{+\infty}{\mathbb{P}}\bigg\{\|\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{t}(\{{\mathbf{X}}_{l},Y_{l}\}_{l=1}^{t})-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|\geq c\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\lambda_{\min}}\cdot\frac{d+\log(t)}{t}\bigg\}=+\infty,

so the
\log(t)/t
term in Theorem
1
is optimal, up to constants. Even when
\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{t}
are independent, the second Borel-Cantelli lemma implies

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\bigg\{\|\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{t}(\{{\mathbf{X}}_{l},Y_{l}\}_{l=1}^{t})-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|\geq c\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\lambda_{\min}}\cdot\frac{d+\log(t)}{t},\,{\rm i.o.}\bigg\}=1.

Remark 1

(Tail Probability Accumulation)
.

Non-asymptotic analyses for online estimation and SGD typically apply concentration bounds at each time step, since data are processed sequentially without storage. This inherently leads to accumulation of tail probabilities
(
35
;
31
;
43
;
57
;
44
)
. Theorem
1
advances the state of the art by accommodating an infinite horizon
T\leq+\infty
, whereas earlier results were restricted to horizons of size at most
T\leq C\exp(d)
(e.g.,
57
). Enabling a non-asymptotic analysis for potentially unbounded horizons is a substantial and nontrivial improvement.

Remark 2

(Regret)
.

Various definitions of regret exist for online linear regression. Typically, regret measures cumulative predictive performance rather than estimation quality at a single time point. Since Theorem
1
ensures optimal estimation error uniformly for all sufficiently large
t
, the corresponding regret is also statistically optimal. To avoid confusion with bandit-style regret definitions, we omit further discussion here.

We now turn to the asymptotic behavior of
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}
under Markovian covariates and noise with convergent second moments. The result shows that SGD iterates converge in distribution to a multivariate Gaussian. We use
O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(\cdot)
to denote stochastic boundedness.

Theorem 2

(Asymptotic Performance)
.

Assume
{\mathbb{E}}\{{\mathbf{X}}_{t}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}=0
,
{\mathbb{E}}\{\xi_{t}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{+}\}=0
, and
\xi_{t}\perp{\mathbf{X}}_{t}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
. Suppose there exist
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{*}
and
\sigma^{*}
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to+\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\big\{\big\|{\mathbb{E}}\{{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}{\mathbf{X}}_{t}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{*}\big\|\big\}=0,\,\text{ and }\lim_{t\to+\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\big\{\big|{\mathbb{E}}\{\xi_{t}^{2}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}-\sigma_{*}^{2}\big|\big\}=0.

Additionally, suppose there exist
\lambda_{\max},\lambda_{\min},\sigma>0
such that

\lambda_{\min}{\mathbf{I}}\preceq{\mathbb{E}}\{{\mathbf{X}}_{t}{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\},\quad\displaystyle\sup_{{\mathbf{V}}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}:\,{\mathbf{V}}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{t}}{\mathbb{E}}\{({\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}{\mathbf{V}})^{4}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}\leq\lambda_{\max}^{2},\quad{\mathbb{E}}\{\xi_{t}^{4}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{+}\}\leq\sigma^{4}\quad\text{a.s.}

With stepsize
\eta_{t}=\frac{C_{a}/\lambda_{\min}}{t-t_{1}+C_{b}d}
, where
C_{a}\geq 2
and
C_{b}\geq 3C_{a}^{2}(\lambda_{\max}^{2}/\lambda_{\min}^{2})
, we have the decomposition

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}=\sum_{j=0}^{t}\prod_{l=j+1}^{t}\left({\mathbf{I}}-\eta_{l}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{*}\right)\eta_{j}\cdot\xi_{j}\cdot{\mathbf{X}}_{j}+{\mathbf{R}}_{t},

where
R_{t}=O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1/t)
. Moreover,

\sqrt{t}\sum_{j=0}^{t}\prod_{l=j+1}^{t}\left({\mathbf{I}}-\eta_{l}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{*}\right)\eta_{j}\cdot\xi_{j}\cdot{\mathbf{X}}_{j}\rightsquigarrow N(\boldsymbol{0},{\mathbf{U}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{C_{a}}^{*}{\mathbf{U}}^{*\top}),

where
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{*}={\mathbf{U}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{*}{\mathbf{U}}^{*\top}
is the eigen-decomposition,
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{*}=\operatorname{diag}\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{d}\}
, and

\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{C_{a}}^{*}=\operatorname{diag}\left\{\frac{\sigma_{*}^{2}}{\lambda_{i}}\cdot\frac{(C_{a}\lambda_{i}/\lambda_{\min})^{2}}{2C_{a}\lambda_{i}/\lambda_{\min}-1}\right\}.

Theorem
2
characterizes the asymptotic distribution of SGD iterates under dependent covariates and noise, encompassing the i.i.d. Gaussian case. The limiting covariance reflects an interplay between
\lambda_{i}
and
\lambda_{\min}
. Note also that the limit law depends on
C_{a}
but not on
C_{b}
: asymptotically,
t+C_{b}d=O(t)
, whereas Theorem
1
requires a sufficiently large
C_{b}
to ensure sharp non-asymptotic bounds and tail probabilities. The bandit setting (Section
4.3
) extends Theorem
2
; its proof is therefore omitted and we defer our comparisons with existing literature to Section
4.4
. For related high-dimensional inference results under i.i.d. data and stepsize decaying as
1/t^{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in(1/2,1)
, we refer to
1
and
56
.

3
Sparse Linear Regression with Dependent Data

In this section, we study sparse linear regression in the presence of dependent covariates and noise. The covariates and responses satisfy the same linear model as in Section
2
. In contrast to that setting, the unknown parameter vector
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}
is now assumed to lie in a low-dimensional subspace induced by a sparsity constraint; specifically,

\displaystyle Y_{t}={\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}+\xi_{t},\quad\text{with}\quad|\operatorname{supp}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})|\leq s.

We impose the following assumption on the covariates, which is weaker than Assumption
2
due to the sparsity of
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}
.

Assumption 3
.

The covariate
{\mathbf{X}}_{t}
satisfies
{\mathbb{E}}\{{\mathbf{X}}_{t}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}=0
almost surely. For some
s\leq d
, there exists
\lambda_{\min}>0
such that

\min_{{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq[d]:\,1\leq|{\mathcal{S}}|\leq s}\lambda_{\min}\Big({\mathbb{E}}\big\{[{\mathbf{X}}_{t}]_{{\mathcal{S}}}[{\mathbf{X}}_{t}]_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{\top}\big|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\big\}\Big)\geq\lambda_{\min},\quad{\rm a.s.},

and there exists
\lambda_{\max}>0
such that

\displaystyle\max_{{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq[d]:\,|{\mathcal{S}}|\leq s}\big\|[{\mathbf{X}}_{t}]_{{\mathcal{S}}}\big|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\big\|_{\Psi_{2}}^{2}\leq\lambda_{\max},\quad{\rm a.s.}.

where
\lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max}
are constants.

This assumption differs from the dense setting in Assumption
1
: here the restricted eigenvalue condition is imposed only on
s
-dimensional subvectors, making it strictly weaker. Furthermore, Assumption
3
guarantees the existence of finite constants
\lambda_{\max,s}^{\textsf{Off}},\lambda_{\max,1}^{\textsf{Off}}\geq 0
such that

\displaystyle\max_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}\subseteq[d]:|{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}|\leq s}\;\max_{{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq[d]:|{\mathcal{S}}|\leq s}\|[{\mathbf{X}}_{t}]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}}[{\mathbf{X}}_{t}]_{{\mathcal{S}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}}^{\top}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\|_{\Psi_{1}}\leq\lambda_{\max,s}^{\textsf{Off}},\quad\max_{i\notin{\mathcal{S}}^{*}}\;\max_{{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq[d]:|{\mathcal{S}}|\leq s}\|[{\mathbf{X}}_{t}]_{i}[{\mathbf{X}}_{t}]_{{\mathcal{S}}\setminus i}^{\top}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\|_{\Psi_{1}}\leq\lambda_{\max,1}^{\textsf{Off}}.

These quantities measure correlations among subsets of covariates. They vanish, for example, when any two entries of the vector
{\mathbf{X}}_{t}
have disjoint supports. For simplicity, we assume

\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|^{2}\leq C\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\lambda_{\max}},

which can be ensured by an offline initialization or by running a short warm-up SGD phase with either a constant stepsize (as in Theorem
1
) or the
\frac{C_{a}}{t+C_{b}d}
stepsize scheme described in footnote
1
.

Our analysis proceeds in three steps. Section
3.1
studies the behavior of SGD under a fixed support, highlighting its dependence on
\lambda_{\max,s}^{\textsf{Off}}
even if the initial support does not include the true support. Section
3.2
introduces a statistic for support recovery and establishes conditions under which missing support elements can be detected. Section
3.3
integrates the estimation and support-recovery procedures into a unified algorithm. The key observation is that while a single data point suffices for estimation updates, accurate support recovery fundamentally requires aggregating historical information.

3.1
Sparse SGD: Fixed Support

In this section, we analyze the behavior of sparse SGD when the support is fixed throughout the iterations. Let
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
denote the initial support set and
{\mathcal{S}}^{*}:=\operatorname{supp}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})
be the true support. Importantly, we impose no structural relationship between
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
. In particular, neither
|{\mathcal{S}}^{*}|=|{\mathcal{S}}_{0}|
nor in the inclusion
{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
is required. This stands in sharp contrast to settings such as
25
, where the initial support is assumed to contain the true one.

The update rule operates as follows. At each iteration, the algorithm first computes a stochastic gradient using the newly received data point, updates the current estimate accordingly, and then projects the iterate back on the fixed support
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
by zeroing out all other coordinates. Formally, the update is given by

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t+1}={\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\big({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}-\eta_{t}\cdot{\mathbf{g}}_{t}\big),

(5)

where
{\mathbf{g}}_{t}:=({\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}-Y_{t}){\mathbf{X}}_{t}
is the instantaneous gradient and
{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}
denotes the hard-thresholding operator that retains only the coordinates in
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
. Although the support remains fixed, this scheme differs substantially from the dense setting considered in Section
2
. Even when
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
is misspecified, the estimation error on
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
can be affected by components in
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
due to correlations among the subvectors
[{\mathbf{X}}_{t}]_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}
,
[{\mathbf{X}}_{t}]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}
, and
[{\mathbf{X}}_{t}]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{*}}
. The next lemma makes this dependence explicit and illustrates how misspecification of the support influences convergence.

Lemma 1
.

Suppose Assumptions
2
and
3
hold with
s
where
|{\mathcal{S}}_{0}|\leq s
and
|{\mathcal{S}}^{*}|\leq s
. Consider the stepsize
\eta_{t}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}}\frac{C_{a}}{t+C_{b}s\log(2d/s)}
with
C_{a}\geq 2
and
C_{b}\geq C(C_{a}\lambda_{\max}/\lambda_{\min})^{2}
. Then,

\displaystyle\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{c}}\|^{2}=\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\|^{2},

and for any tail-probability sequence
\{\delta_{t}\}
satisfying the requirements in Theorem
1
, with probability at least
1-\sum_{l=0}^{t}\exp(-c\{s\log(2d/s)+l/C_{a}\})-\sum_{l=0}^{t}\exp(-c\{s\log(2d/s)+\delta_{l}\})
, we have

\displaystyle\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t+1}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\|^{2}\leq\frac{C^{*}}{\lambda_{\min}}\frac{s\log(2d/s)+\delta_{t+1}}{t+1+C_{b}s\log(2d/s)}\sigma^{2}+C\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{\max,s}^{\textsf{Off}}}{\lambda_{\min}}\bigg)^{2}\cdot\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\|^{2}.

where
C^{*}=CC_{a}^{2}\lambda_{\max}/\lambda_{\min}+C_{b}\lambda_{\min}/\lambda_{\max}
.

This result highlights two key phenomena. First, the estimation error on
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
decays at the minimax rate as in the dense case, if
{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
. Second, and more importantly, the second term reveals the persistent effect of omitted relevant covariates. When
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
fails to contain the full true support, correlations captured by
\lambda_{\max,s}^{\textsf{Off}}
introduce a nonvanishing bias term that cannot be eliminated through additional iterations. This underscores the necessity of a support-recovery mechanism, developed in Section
3.2
.

3.2
Sparse SGD: Support Recovery

As shown in Lemma
1
, a fixed but misspecified support inevitably induces a persistent bias. It is therefore essential to adaptively recover the support of
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}
. In this section, we develop a support-update mechanism based on a statistic that aggregates the interaction between the covariates and the residuals over a given index set. For any index set
{\mathcal{T}}
(e.g.,
{\mathcal{T}}=\{1,\ldots,T\}
), define

\displaystyle{\mathbf{G}}(\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t},{\mathbf{X}}_{t},Y_{t}\}_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}):=\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}({\mathbf{X}}_{t}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}-Y_{t}){\mathbf{X}}_{t}.

The vector
{\mathbf{G}}(\cdot)
encodes the alignment between the residuals and the covariates and coincides with the sum of stochastic gradients under squared loss. Importantly, even when other loss functions are used, this quantity remains informative. We will show that
{\mathbf{G}}
is particularly effective in identifying coordinates in
{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
, thereby revealing missing components of the true support. To make this role explicit, we expand
{\mathbf{G}}
using the model structure:

\displaystyle{\mathbf{G}}(\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t},{\mathbf{X}}_{t},Y_{t}\}_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}})=\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}{\mathbf{X}}_{t}{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})-\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\xi_{t}\cdot{\mathbf{X}}_{t}.

We say that the sequence
\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}\}_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}
has a common support
{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
if
[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}]_{{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}^{c}}=\boldsymbol{0}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
. For such a sequence, we introduce the event

{\mathcal{E}}_{\{{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{T}}},V_{{\mathcal{T}}},W_{{\mathcal{T}}}\}}:=\big\{\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|\leq V_{t},\,\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}}\|\leq W_{t},~{\rm for~all}~t\in{\mathcal{T}}\big\},

where
V_{t}
and
W_{t}
are deterministic bounds (specified later) controlling the global and restricted estimation errors along the trajectory. The next lemma establishes lower and upper bounds on the magnitudes of entries of
{\mathbf{G}}
when restricted to different subsets of coordinates. These bounds create a separation that enables reliable support detection.

Lemma 2
.

Suppose Assumptions
2
and
3
hold. Let
\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}\}_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}
be a sequence with common support
{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
, where
|{\mathcal{T}}|\geq Cs
and
|{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}|\leq s
. Under the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{\{{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{T}}},V_{\mathcal{T}},W_{\mathcal{T}}\}}
, for any
u,\delta>0
, the following statements hold:

•

(Lower bound on uncovered support coordinates) For the coordinates in
{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
,

\|[{\mathbf{G}}(\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t},{\mathbf{X}}_{t},Y_{t}\}_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}})]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}}\|\geq|{\mathcal{T}}|\cdot\lambda_{\min}\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}}\|-\lambda_{\max,s}^{\textsf{Off}}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}W_{t}\\
-u-C\sigma\sqrt{|{\mathcal{T}}|}\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}}\sqrt{|{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}|\log(2d/|{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}|)+\delta}

with probability at least

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle-\exp\left(Cs-\min\left\{\frac{u^{2}}{(\lambda_{\max,s}^{\textsf{Off}})^{2}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}W_{t}^{2}+|{\mathcal{T}}|\lambda_{\max}^{2}\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{T}}}\|^{2}},\right.\right.

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\left.\left.\frac{u}{\lambda_{\max,s}^{\textsf{Off}}\max_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}W_{t}+\lambda_{\max}\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{T}}}\|}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle-\exp\left(-c\min\left\{\delta,\sqrt{\frac{\delta|{\mathcal{T}}|}{|{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}|\log(2d/|{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}|)}}\right\}\right).

•

(Upper bound on irrelevant coordinates) For the coordinates in
{\mathcal{S}}^{*c}\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}^{c}
,

\displaystyle\|[{\mathbf{G}}(\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t},{\mathbf{X}}_{t},Y_{t}\}_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}})]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{*c}\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}^{c}}\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda_{\max,1}^{\textsf{Off}}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}V_{t}+u+C\sigma\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}}\sqrt{\delta|{\mathcal{T}}|}

with probability at least

1-d\exp\left(-\min\left\{\frac{u^{2}}{(\lambda_{\max,1}^{\textsf{Off}})^{2}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}V_{t}^{2}},\frac{u}{2\lambda_{\max,1}\max_{t}V_{t}}\right\}\right)-d\exp\Big(-\min\big\{\delta,\sqrt{|{\mathcal{T}}|\delta}\big\}\Big).

Lemma
2
shows that the entries of
{\mathbf{G}}
corresponding to missing true-support coordinates (
{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
) grow at order
\Theta(|{\mathcal{T}}|)
, whereas those on inactive coordinates (
{\mathcal{S}}^{*c}\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}^{c}
) remain much smaller. This separation is crucial for correctly identifying missing support elements. To guarantee a nontrivial gap between these bounds, and hence ensure detectable separation, we require the following condition on the covariates.

Assumption 4
.

The covariates
{\mathbf{X}}_{t}
satisfy

\lambda_{\min}\geq C\sqrt{s}\lambda_{\max,1}^{\textsf{Off}}~~\mbox{ and }~~\lambda_{\min}\geq C\lambda_{\max,s}^{\textsf{Off}},

where the constants
(\lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max,1}^{\textsf{Off}},\lambda_{\max,s}^{\textsf{Off}})
are such that
\displaystyle\min_{{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq[d],\,1\leq|{\mathcal{S}}|\leq s}\lambda_{\min}({\mathbb{E}}\{[{\mathbf{X}}_{t}]_{{\mathcal{S}}}[{\mathbf{X}}_{t}]_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{\top}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\})\geq\lambda_{\min}
,
\displaystyle\max_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}\subseteq[d]:|{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}|\leq s}\;\max_{{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq[d]:|{\mathcal{S}}|\leq s}\|[{\mathbf{X}}_{t}]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}}[{\mathbf{X}}_{t}]_{{\mathcal{S}}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}}^{\top}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\|_{\Psi_{1}}\leq\lambda_{\max,s}^{\textsf{Off}}
and
\displaystyle\max_{i\notin{\mathcal{S}}^{*}}\;\max_{{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq[d]:|{\mathcal{S}}|\leq s}\|[{\mathbf{X}}_{t}]_{i}[{\mathbf{X}}_{t}]_{{\mathcal{S}}\setminus i}^{\top}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\|_{\Psi_{1}}\leq\lambda_{\max,1}^{\textsf{Off}}
.

Assumption
4
requires different entries of
{\mathbf{X}}_{t}
to have correlation bounded by
\lambda_{\min}
, which in fact is equivalent to the conditions in classic offline sparse linear regression
(
72
)
. Lemma
1
provides valid choices for the sequences
\{V_{t}\}
and
\{W_{t}\}
under the update rule (
5
). Specifically,

\displaystyle W_{t}:=\frac{\sqrt{C^{*}}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}}}\sqrt{\frac{s\log(2d/s)+\delta_{t}}{t+C_{b}s\log(2d/s)}}\sigma+C\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{\max,s}^{\textsf{Off}}}{\lambda_{\min}}\bigg)\cdot\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\|

and

\displaystyle V_{t}:=\frac{\sqrt{C^{*}}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}}}\sqrt{\frac{s\log(2d/s)+\delta_{t}}{t+C_{b}s\log(2d/s)}}\sigma+C\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{\max,s}^{\textsf{Off}}}{\lambda_{\min}}\bigg)\cdot\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\|+\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\|.

Substituting
{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}={\mathcal{S}}_{0}
,
{\mathcal{T}}=\{0,1,\ldots,t\}
, and the bounds above into Lemma
2
yields the following corollary.

Corollary 1
.

Suppose Assumptions
2
,
3
, and
4
hold. Take
\delta_{l}=C\log(l)+\log(d/s)
and suppose that the estimation error dynamics in Theorem
1
holds. Then, for any
t
satisfying

\frac{t}{\log(t)}\geq C|{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0}|\log\left(\frac{2d}{|{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0}|}\right)\cdot\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\lambda_{\min}\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\|^{2}},

with probability at least
1-C(td/s)^{-100}
,

\displaystyle\left\|[{\mathbf{G}}(\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t},{\mathbf{X}}_{t},Y_{t}\}_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}})]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\right\|>\sqrt{|{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0}|}\cdot\left\|[{\mathbf{G}}(\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t},{\mathbf{X}}_{t},Y_{t}\}_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}})]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{*c}\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{c}}\right\|_{\infty},

which in turn implies that
{\rm argmax}_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}^{c}}\big|[{\mathbf{G}}(\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t},{\mathbf{X}}_{t},Y_{t}\}_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}})]_{i}\big|\in{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
.

Corollary
1
shows that once
t
is sufficiently large, the statistic
{\mathbf{G}}
reliably distinguishes the missing coordinates of the true support, enabling accurate support recovery. We now proceed to integrate this selection mechanism with the estimation updates.

3.3
Sparse SGD: Estimation and Support Recovery

Intuitively, each incoming observation contributes to reducing the estimation error, but a single observations is insufficient for reliable variable selection. In contrast, aggregating information across historical data, via the summary statistic
{\mathbf{G}}
, enables us to identify missing support elements once the sample size is large enough. This motivates an iterative procedure that alternates between (i) estimation on a fixed support and (ii) support expansion using
{\mathbf{G}}
. We present below an algorithm that integrates estimation and support recovery in an online fashion.

Algorithm.
Over times
t\in\{0,1,\ldots,T\}
, where
T
may be
+\infty
, choose update times
\tau_{0}=0
,
0<\tau_{1}<\cdots<\tau_{\alpha}
. During each interval
[\tau_{l-1},\tau_{l}-1]
, the iterates
\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}\}
share a common support
{\mathcal{S}}_{l}
and evolve according to the sparse update rule

{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t+1}={\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{l}}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}-\eta_{t}\cdot{\mathbf{g}}_{t}),

where
{\mathbf{g}}_{t}=({\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t}-Y_{t}){\mathbf{X}}_{t}
denotes the stochastic gradient and
{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{l}}
is the hard-thresholding operator projecting onto support
{\mathcal{S}}_{l}
. The sequence of supports satisfies
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}
, and the update at stage
l
is performed via

{\mathcal{S}}_{l}={\mathcal{S}}_{l-1}\cup\big\{{\rm argmax}_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{l-1}^{c}}\big|[{\mathbf{G}}(\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t},{\mathbf{X}}_{t},Y_{t}\}_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}_{l}})]_{i}\big|\big\},

where
{\mathcal{T}}_{l}\subseteq[t]
denotes the set of times indices used to compute the statistic
{\mathbf{G}}
at step
l
. The choice of
\{\tau_{l}\}
and
\{{\mathcal{T}}_{l}\}
is not unique and will be discussed in detail below. We consider two natural choices for the index set
{\mathcal{T}}_{l}
: (1) a local window,
{\mathcal{T}}_{l}:=\{\tau_{l-1},\ldots,\tau_{l}-1\}
, and (2) a cumulative window,
{\mathcal{T}}_{l}:=\{0,1,\ldots,\tau_{l}-1\}
. From a different perspective, if each update enlarges the support by exactly one element in
{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
, then full recovery of the true support requires at most
|{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0}|
times. The following proposition characterizes the estimation error dynamics under this integrated update scheme.

Proposition 1
.

Suppose Assumptions
2
,
3
, and
4
hold. Let the stepsize scheme be
\eta_{t}=\frac{C_{a}/\lambda_{\min}}{t+C_{b}s\log(2d/s)}
, with constants satisfying
C_{a}\geq 5
and
C_{b}\geq C(C_{a}\lambda_{\max}/\lambda_{\min})^{2}
. Assume also that
{\mathcal{S}}_{l}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{l-1}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
for all
l\in\{1,2,\ldots,\alpha\}
and that the tail probability sequence
\{\delta_{l}\}
satisfies the conditions in Theorem
1
. Then, for any
t\in[\tau_{l},\tau_{l+1}-1]
, with probability at least
1-\sum_{l=0}^{t}\exp(-c\{s\log(2d/s)+l/C_{a}\})-\sum_{l=0}^{t}\exp(-c\{s\log(2d/s)+\delta_{l}\})
, the following bound holds:

\displaystyle\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t+1}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}_{l}}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{s\log(2d/s)+\delta_{t+1}}{t+1+C_{b}s\log(2d/s)}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\lambda_{\min}}+C\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{\max,s}^{\textsf{Off}}}{\lambda_{\min}}\bigg)^{2}\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{l}}\|^{2}

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~+C\sum_{i=1}^{l}\bigg(\frac{\tau_{i}+C_{b}s\log(2d/s)}{t+1+C_{b}s\log(2d/s)}\bigg)^{C_{a}-2}\cdot\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}_{i}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{i-1}}\|^{2}.

Proposition
1
quantifies the interplay between support recovery and estimation accuracy. The second term in the bound disappears if either
\lambda_{\max,s}^{\textsf{Off}}=0
or the current support already contains the true support, i.e.,
{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}_{t}
. The third term reveals a key design principle: early and accurate inclusion of missing support coordinates is crucial for minimizing overall estimation error. However, Corollary
1
shows that accurate support detection requires a sufficiently large sample size within the window
{\mathcal{T}}_{l}
. Thus, the timing of support updates must balance two competing considerations. Updating too late slows convergence because unselected true coordinates remain uncorrected, whereas updating too early risks selecting incorrect variables before the statistic
{\mathbf{G}}
has achieved reliable separation. By combining Proposition
1
with Lemma
2
, we now obtain an integrated guarantee for simultaneous estimation and support recovery.

Theorem 3
.

Suppose Assumptions
2
,
3
, and
4
hold. Let the stepsize scheme be
\eta_{t}=\frac{C_{a}/\lambda_{\min}}{t+C_{b}s\log(2d/s)}
with
C_{a}\geq 5
and
C_{b}\geq C(C_{a}\lambda_{\max}/\lambda_{\min})^{2}
. Assume the initial estimate satisfies
\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|^{2}\leq\sigma^{2}/\lambda_{\min}
, and let
s_{+}:=|{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0}|
. Suppose the support update times
\{\tau_{l}\}
and index sets
\{{\mathcal{T}}_{l}\}
satisfy:

1.

\tau_{1}/\log(\tau_{1})\geq Cs_{+}\log(d/s_{+})\cdot\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\lambda_{\min}\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\|^{2}}
;

2.

For each
l\in\{2,\ldots,s_{+}\}
, let
s_{l}:=|{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{l}|
.

(a)

If
{\mathcal{T}}_{l}=\{\tau_{l-1},\ldots,\tau_{l}-1\}
(local window), then require

\tau_{l}\geq\max\left\{\left(\frac{C}{\tau_{l-1}^{C_{a}-4}\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{l}}\|^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{l-1}\tau_{i}^{C_{a}-2}\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}_{i}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{i-1}}\|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},\tau_{l-1}+\frac{Cs_{l}\log(2d/s_{l})\cdot\sigma^{2}}{\lambda_{\min}\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{l}}\|^{2}}\right\},

(b)

If
{\mathcal{T}}_{l}=\{0,1,\ldots,\tau_{l}-1\}
(cumulative window), then require

\tau_{l}\geq\max\left\{\frac{C}{\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{l}}\|}\sum_{i=1}^{l-1}\tau_{i}\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}_{i}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{i-1}}\|,\frac{Cs_{l}\log(2d/s_{l})\cdot\sigma^{2}}{\lambda_{\min}\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{l}}\|^{2}}\right\}.

Then, with probability at least
1-Cd^{-100}
, for all
t\in[\tau_{s_{0}},+\infty]
, we have
{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}_{t}
, and the estimation error satisfies

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{t+1}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C^{*}\frac{s\log(2d/s)}{t+1+C_{b}s\log(2d/s)}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\lambda_{\min}}

\displaystyle~~~~+C\sum_{i=1}^{s_{0}}\bigg(\frac{\tau_{i}+C_{b}s\log(2d/s)}{t+1+C_{b}s\log(2d/s)}\bigg)^{C_{a}-2}\|[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}]_{{\mathcal{S}}_{i}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{i-1}}\|^{2}.

Theorem
3
shows once the support is fully recovered, the estimation error decays at the statistically optimal rate. The contributions from earlier support-mismatch stages diminish rapidly owing to the exponent
C_{a}-2
, and the long-term performance matches the minimax-optimal rate for sparse linear regression under dependent data. In practice, one may update the support whenever the statistic
{\mathbf{G}}
exhibits clear
O(t)
growth on coordinates outside the current support, as suggested by Corollary
1
. Theorem
3
is numerically verified in Section
5
. To this end, our procedure provides a computationally efficient alternative to existing approaches such as online LASSO algorithms
(
70
;
32
)
, which require solving a regularized optimization problem and maintaining
O(d^{2})
summary statistics at each step.

4
Contextual Linear Bandit with Dependent Data

We now turn to the online contextual linear bandit problem. Relative to the regression setting, this problem introduces an additional and fundamental source of dependence: beyond the inherent dependence in the data stream
({\mathbf{X}}_{t},\xi_{t})
, the sequence of observations is now adaptively generated through the decision-making policy. Each action taken at time
t
depends on the current estimate, which in turn depends on all past outcomes. This feedback loop creates a twofold dependence structure, temporal dependence in the covariates and noise, and adaptive dependence induced by exploration-exploitation decisions. Our goal in this section is to show that the proposed SGD framework is sufficiently robust to handle this full dependence structure. In particular, the same techniques developed for dependent linear regression can be integrated with an
\varepsilon
-greedy exploration strategy to achieve simultaneously (i) statistically optimal estimation error for each arm and (ii) statistically optimal regret. This extends classical analyses for i.i.d. covariates and noise
(
29
;
28
;
5
;
4
;
13
;
14
;
21
)
to the substantially more general dependent-data setting.

At each time
t
, the decision-maker observes a covariate vector
{\mathbf{X}}_{t}
and must select one of the
K
arms. Each arm
i\in[K]
is parameterized by an unknown vector
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}
. Pulling arm
i
yields the reward

Y_{t}={\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}+\xi_{t}
, where
\xi_{t}
denotes the noise term, which is allowed to be dependent. Let
a_{t}
denote the arm selected at time
t
. Over a (possibly infinite) horizon
T
, the goal is to maximize the cumulative reward, or equivalently, to minimize the regret

\displaystyle\textsf{Regret}(T):={\mathbb{E}}\left\{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\max_{i\in[K]}{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}-{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{a_{t}}^{*}\right)\right\}.

(6)

Equivalently, the objective is to learn a decision rule for
a_{t}
that minimizes regret, or to identify the oracle decision region that maps
{\mathbf{X}}_{t}
to the optimal arm. Although this objective differs from minimizing estimation error at a single time point (as in Section
2
), we will show that our approach achieves both goals: it yields statistically optimal estimation error for each arm and statistically optimal regret. To accomplish this, we consider a natural integration of SGD with an
\varepsilon
-greedy exploration mechanism. The algorithm is given below.

Algorithm

Let
\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{(0)}\}
be arbitrary initial estimates, and let
\{\eta_{t}\}
and
\{\pi_{t}\}
denote the stepsize and exploration probability sequences, respectively. At time
t
, we observe the covariate
{\mathbf{X}}_{t}
and draw
\alpha_{t}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(\pi_{t})
. If
\alpha_{t}=0
(the exploitation step), we pull the arm that maximizes the estimated reward,

a_{t}\in\arg\max_{i\in[K]}{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{(t)}.

If
\alpha_{t}=1
(the exploration step), we select an arm uniformly at random:

a_{t}\sim\mathrm{Unif}\{1,2,\ldots,K\}.

After pulling arm , we observe the reward
Y_{t}
, and update only the selected arm using SGD:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{(t+1)}={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{(t)}-\eta_{t}\ {\mathbb{I}}_{\{a_{t}=i\}}\cdot({\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{(t)}-Y_{t})\ {\mathbf{X}}_{t},\quad i\in[K].

(7)

Remark that
\{\pi_{t}\}
and
\{\eta_{t}\}
are to be specified.

Denote
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}=\sigma(Y_{t-1},\alpha_{t},{\mathbf{X}}_{t-1},\ldots,Y_{0},\alpha_{0},{\mathbf{X}}_{0})
. Clearly,
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{(t)}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
. Define the augmented
\sigma
-field
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{+}=\sigma({\mathbf{X}}_{t},Y_{t-1},\alpha_{t},{\mathbf{X}}_{t-1},\ldots,Y_{0},\alpha_{0},{\mathbf{X}}_{0})
, so that
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{+}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}_{t+1}
. At time
t
, with probability
\pi_{t}
, the algorithm selects an arm uniformly at random from
[K]
; this constitutes the exploration step and is used to acquire information about all arms. With probability
1-\pi_{t}
, the algorithm selects the arm that maximizes the estimated reward based on past data; this is the exploitation step. The exploration rate
\pi_{t}
governs the fundamental trade-off: full exploration yields the smallest estimation error but incurs large regret, whereas pure exploitation risks committing to incorrect arms. Consequently,
\pi_{t}
influences both the estimation accuracy of each
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}
and the cumulative regret. In this work, we allow for a broad class of exploration schedules, including functionally defined choices of
\pi_{t}
as formalized in Definition
3
. The stepsize sequence
\{\eta_{t}\}
serves as an additional algorithmic parameter to be specified.

Remark 3

(
K=2
versus
K\geq 2
)
.

Throughout, we consider
K\geq 2
. Importantly, the multi-arm case
K>2
is not a trivial extension of the two-arm setting. When
K=2
, each arm is optimal for a nonempty region of covariates. In contrast, when
K\geq 3
, some arms may be globally sub-optimal, never achieving maximal reward for any covariate. For instance, let
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}^{*}=(1,0)^{\top}
,
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}^{*}=(0.1,0)^{\top}
,
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{3}^{*}=(-1,0)^{\top}
,
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{4}^{*}=(0,1)^{\top}
,
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{5}^{*}=(0,-1)^{\top}
. Then for every
{\mathbf{X}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, arm 2 is never optimal. Such sub-optimal arms introduce additional complexity that does not arise when
K=2

(
14
;
13
)
.

4.1
Decision Region Characterizations

In this section, we introduce a conic approximation of the arm-specific decision regions. For arm
i
, the oracle decision region is defined as

{\mathcal{U}}_{i}^{*}=\bigg\{{\mathbf{X}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}:\ {\mathbf{X}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}>\max_{j\neq i}{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j}^{*}\bigg\}.

It is straightforward to verify that
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}^{*}
is either a cone or an empty set. Indeed, if
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{i}^{*}
, then for any positive scalar
a
, we have
a{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{i}^{*}
. To minimize the regret, we need to estimate and approximate the oracle region
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}^{*}
. A line of influential studies
(
29
;
64
;
5
;
33
;
15
;
4
)
assume that the covariates lie in a bounded domain and approximate
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}^{*}
using bounded polyhedral regions constructed from separating hyperplanes. Specifically, they consider

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i}(h;D):=\bigg\{\|{\mathbf{X}}\|\leq D:\ {\mathbf{X}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}-\max_{j\neq i}{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j}^{*}\geq h\bigg\}.

(8)

The set
\widetilde{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i}(h;D)
excludes the entire neighborhood of the origin. To address this limitation, we propose an alternative characterization, which we term the conic approximation of the decision region:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h):=\bigg\{{\mathbf{X}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}:\ \frac{{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}-\max_{j\neq i}{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j}^{*}}{\|{\mathbf{X}}\|}\geq h\bigg\}.

(9)

The region
\mathcal{U}_{i}(h)
is again either conic or empty, mirroring the geometric structure of
\mathcal{U}_{i}^{*}
. Moreover, the family
\mathcal{U}_{i}(h)
naturally accommodates unbounded covariate support and retains coverage of neighborhoods near the origin. This feature is particularly advantageous in settings where the covariate distribution is highly concentrated around the origin, in which case
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h;D)
may discard substantial probability mass by removing the entire origin. In contrast, when covariates take values from a discrete set, the two constructions
\mathcal{U}_{i}(h)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h;D)
coincide. To illustrate the geometric differences, Figure
1
depicts the bounded approximation
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h;D)
and the conic approximation
\mathcal{U}_{i}(h)
. The next lemma formally characterizes the relationships between
\mathcal{U}_{i}^{*}
,
\mathcal{U}_{i}(h)
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h;D)
, and establishes several key geometric properties of the proposed conic region.

(a)

Region of
\widetilde{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i}

(b)

Region of
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}

Figure 1
:
Two approximations of the oracle decision region
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}^{*}
. The left panel illustrates the bounded polyhedral approximation
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
, shown as the shaded region enclosed by several line segments. The right panel shows the conic approximation
\mathcal{U}_{i}
, which forms an unbounded, scale-invariant region.

Lemma 3
.

For any
h>0
and
D>0
, the following set relations hold:

(i)

For
\widetilde{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i}(h;D)
, it has
\widetilde{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i}(h;D)\subsetneq{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h/D)\cap\{{\mathbf{X}}:\|{\mathbf{X}}\|\leq D\}\subsetneq{\mathcal{U}}_{i}^{*}\cap\{{\mathbf{X}}:\|{\mathbf{X}}\|\leq D\}
.

(ii)

Conversely, it has
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h)\cap\{{\mathbf{X}}:\|{\mathbf{X}}\|\leq D\}\nsubseteq\widetilde{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i}(hD;D)
.

(iii)

Moreover,
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h)\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}_{i}^{*}
, and in particular
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(0)
is the closure of
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}^{*}
. For any
h_{1}>h_{2}
, it has
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h_{1})\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h_{2})
.

Furthermore, if
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}^{*}\neq\emptyset
, then for any

h\in\bigg(0,\,\min_{i\in{\mathcal{A}}}\sup_{\|{\mathbf{z}}\|=1}({\mathbf{z}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}-\max_{j\neq i}{\mathbf{z}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j}^{*})\bigg),

we have
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h)\neq\emptyset
. Moreover, if
{\mathcal{U}}_{j}^{*}=\emptyset
, then

\displaystyle{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j}^{*}\leq\max_{i\neq j}{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}~~\text{for all }~{\mathbf{X}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.

Proof.

We prove only part (i). For brevity, write
{\mathcal{X}}:=\left\{{\mathbf{X}}:\|{\mathbf{X}}\|\leq D\right\}
. Clearly,
\widetilde{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i}(h;D)\subsetneq{\mathcal{X}}
. We show
\widetilde{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i}(h;D)\subsetneq{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h/D)
. For any
{\mathbf{x}}\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i}(h;D)
,
{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}-\max_{j\neq i}{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j}^{*}\geq h\geq\frac{h}{D}\|{\mathbf{x}}\|
, implying
\widetilde{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i}(h;D)\subset{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h/D)
. Conversely, the vector
\frac{h}{2({\mathbf{x}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}-\max_{j\neq i}{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j}^{*})}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}
lies in
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h/D)
but not in
\widetilde{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i}(h;D)
, completing the proof that
\widetilde{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i}(h;{\mathcal{X}})
is strictly contained in
{\mathcal{X}}\cap{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h)
.
∎

Next, let
{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i}}
denote estimators of
{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i}^{*}}
. The corresponding empirical decision region in (
7
) is

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}\left(i,\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j}\}_{j\in[K]}\right):=\bigg\{{\mathbf{X}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}:\;{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}>\max_{j\neq i}{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j}\bigg\}.

(10)

During exploitation steps, the algorithm selects arm
i
whenever
{\mathbf{X}}_{t}\in{\mathcal{X}}(i,\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j}^{(t)}\}_{j\in[K]})
. Importantly,
{\mathcal{X}}(i,\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j}\}_{j\in[K]})
is a conic region: if
\mathbf{X}
belongs to the set, then for any
a>0
,
a\mathbf{X}
also belongs to it. The next result formalizes the relationship between the empirical region and the oracle approximation
\mathcal{U}_{i}
.

Lemma 4
.

If
\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}\|\leq h_{0}
holds for all
i\in[K]
, then we have

{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(2h_{0})\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}\Big(i,\left\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j}\right\}_{j\in[K]}\Big)\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(-2h_{0}).

Lemma
4
links the empirical decision regions used in the update rule (
7
) to the conic approximations of the oracle regions
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}^{*}
, with an explicit and interpretable dependence on the estimation error
\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}\|
. This result is a key structural component of both our finite-sample and asymptotic analyses. It ensures that sufficiently accurate parameter estimates yield decision regions that correctly approximate the true optimal regions, enabling control of both regret and estimation error within a unified framework.

The classical notion of separability between optimal and suboptimal arms in contextual bandits
(
5
;
4
)
is expressed through bounded-domain approximations such as
\widetilde{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i}
. Our next assumption adapts this separability condition to the scale-invariant regions
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h)
.

Assumption 5

(Arm Optimality)
.

There exists
h>0
and a partition
{\mathcal{A}}\cup{\mathcal{A}}^{c}=[K]
such that for all
t
and
i\in{\mathcal{A}}
,
{\mathbb{E}}({\mathbf{X}}_{t}{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}\cdot{\mathbb{I}}\{{\mathbf{X}}_{t}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h)\}\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t})\succeq\lambda_{\min}\cdot{\mathbf{I}}_{d}
, and for every
j\in{\mathcal{A}}^{c}
,

{\mathbb{P}}\bigg({\mathbf{X}}_{t}:\;\frac{\max_{i\in[K]}{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}-{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j}^{*}}{\|{\mathbf{X}}_{t}\|}\geq h\bigg|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\bigg)=1.

Remark 4

(On the Characterization of Optimal Regions)
.

Assumption
5
is formulated using our scale-invariant definition of the optimal decision region,
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h)
in (
9
). This represents a subtle but important departure from conventional formulations in the contextual bandit literature. For example, influential works such as
5
and
4
employ a bounded-domain characterization, which corresponds to our construction of
\widetilde{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i}(h;D)
in (
8
). While such bounded-domain approximations are appropriate when covariates lie within a compact set, they introduce two limitations. First, they require prior knowledge of a bounded support, an assumption that may be violated in many modern applications where covariates are naturally unbounded. Second, they exclude an entire neighborhood around the origin. This exclusion can be problematic for well-behaved designs, such as multivariate Gaussian covariates, whose probability mass is heavily concentrated near the origin. In these settings, removing the region near zero may eliminate a substantial portion of the informative design space, thereby reducing applicability or statistical efficiency. Our definition of
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h)
circumvents these issues. By construction,
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h)
is scale-invariant (i.e., a cone), making it well suited for unbounded covariate distributions while retaining the essential geometric properties of the oracle region
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}^{*}
. Notably,
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h)
involves the origin rather than excluding it, ensuring that no disproportionately large region of high-probability covariate mass is discarded. As a result, Assumption
5
provides a more general and broadly applicable foundation for contextual bandit analysis, allowing our theoretical guarantees to hold under more general data-generating mechanisms than those accommodated by bounded-domain assumptions.

Additionally, if Assumption
5
holds for some
h
, then it automatically holds for any
h^{\prime}\in(0,h)
. In the special case
K=2
, this assumption is always satisfied and thus requires no further conditions. We now proceed to establish the regularity properties of the problem, which form a key component in analyzing the convergence dynamics of the proposed method. The intuition underlying these properties is partly inspired by
5
. Notably, our framework does not require boundedness on
{\mathbf{X}}
or the parameters
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}
, nor does it rely on an i.i.d. assumption on
\{{\mathbf{X}}_{t}\}
.

Lemma 5

(Regularity Properties)
.

Suppose Assumption
5
holds with
h>0
. Then the following properties hold.

1.

If the estimates satisfy
\{\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}\|\leq\frac{h}{2},\ i\in[K]\}
, then with probability one, for any
j\in{\mathcal{A}}^{c}
,
{\mathcal{X}}(j,\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}\}_{i\in[K]})=\emptyset
, that is,

\displaystyle{\rm argmax}_{i\in[K]}{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}\subseteq{\mathcal{A}},\quad{\rm a.s.}

Moreover, for every
i\in{\mathcal{A}}
,

{\mathcal{U}}_{i}\bigg(2\max_{j\in{\mathcal{A}}}\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j}^{*}\|\bigg)\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}\Big(i,\left\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j}\right\}_{j\in[K]}\Big)\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}_{i}\bigg(-2\max_{j\in{\mathcal{A}}}\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j}^{*}\|\bigg).

2.

For any
i\in{\mathcal{A}}
and any
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
,

{\mathbb{E}}\bigg\{{\mathbb{I}}\bigg\{{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}\geq\max_{j}{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j}\bigg\}\cdot\left({\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}-{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}\right)^{2}\bigg|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\bigg\}\\
\geq{\mathbb{I}}\bigg\{\max_{j}\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j}^{*}\|\leq\frac{h}{2}\bigg\}\cdot\lambda_{\min}\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}\|^{2}.

(11)

Lemma
5
shows that when the estimation error satisfies
\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}\|\leq\frac{h}{2}
, the quantity in (
11
) admits a sharp quadratic lower bound in
\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}\|
. Moreover, in contrast to Lemma
3
, under Assumption
5
and the same error condition, the approximation of the empirical decision region depends only on the estimation accuracy of the arms in
{\mathcal{A}}
. Technically, Lemma
5
plays a central role in enabling the exploitation-phase observations to contribute effectively to parameter refinement. When
h^{\prime}<h
, the region
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h^{\prime})
more closely approximates the oracle region
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}^{*}
, compared to
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h)
, and the union
\cup_{i\in[K]}{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(h^{\prime})
covers a larger subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. However, the lemma also indicates that attaining a strictly positive quadratic lower bound in (
11
) requires controlling the estimation error at a scale proportional to
h^{\prime}
. Thus, reducing
h
expands the decision region but simultaneously requires more accurate parameter estimates in order to maintain the curvature lower bound in (
11
). This illustrates an inherent trade-off in selecting
h
: increasing the probability mass
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{U}}_{i})
comes at the cost of weakening the guaranteed quadratic lower bound.

4.2
Non-asymptotic Performances

In this section, we investigate the non-asymptotic statistical performance of the proposed method. A notable feature of our approach is that both the estimation error for the parameters associated with arms in
\mathcal{A}
and the cumulative regret achieve statistically optimal rates, while requiring only minimal storage, specifically, the most recent estimates and the total number of observations collected thus far. We now detail the convergence dynamics underlying this result.

For clarity of exposition, we assume throughout that the initial estimates satisfy
\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{(0)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}\|^{2}\leq C\sigma^{2}/\lambda_{\max}
. Such an initialization can be obtained using either offline procedures or online methods. For example, as shown in Theorem
1
, SGD with a sufficiently small constant stepsize yields an
O(1)
estimation error with linear convergence, thus meeting the above requirement. We next introduce a general prescription for the exploration rate
\pi_{t}
and the associated sequence of tail probabilities
\{\delta_{t}\}
. The following theorem establishes the resulting non-asymptotic estimation error dynamics.

Theorem 4

(Non-asymptotic Error Dynamics with Tail Level
\delta_{t}
)
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
5
hold. Consider the stepsize
\eta_{t}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}}\frac{C_{a}}{t+C_{b}d}
, and let
\pi\in(0,1]
be any constant. For any sequence
\{\delta_{t}\}
satisfying
(i)

0\leq\delta_{t}\leq\delta_{t+1}
and
(ii)

\delta_{t}\leq Ct/C_{b}
, the following statements hold:

1.

If
\pi_{t}\geq\pi
,
C_{a}\geq 2K/\pi
, and
C_{b}\geq 3C_{a}^{2}(\lambda_{\max}^{2}/\lambda_{\min}^{2})
, then with probability at least
1-K\sum_{l=0}^{t}\exp(-c(d+l/C_{a}))-K\sum_{l=0}^{t}\exp(-c(d+\delta_{l}))
, we have for all
i\in[K]
,

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{(t)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}\|^{2}\leq\ \frac{C^{*}}{\lambda_{\min}}\frac{d+\delta_{t}}{t+C_{b}d}\sigma^{2}~~\text{with}~~C^{*}=CC_{a}^{2}\frac{\lambda_{\max}}{\lambda_{\min}}+C_{b}\frac{\lambda_{\min}}{\lambda_{\max}}.

(12)

2.

For any
t\geq t_{1}:=16C^{*}d\sigma^{2}/(\lambda_{\min}h^{2})-C_{b}d
and any exploration schedule
\pi_{t}\in[0,1]
, we have with probability at least
1-|{\mathcal{A}}|\sum_{l=t_{1}}^{t+1}\exp(-c(d+l/C_{a}))-|{\mathcal{A}}|\sum_{l=t_{1}}^{t+1}\exp(-c(d+\delta_{l}))
that for all
i\in{\mathcal{A}}
,

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{(t)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}\|^{2}\leq\frac{C^{**}}{\lambda_{\min}}\frac{d+\delta_{t}}{t+C_{b}d}\sigma^{2}~~\text{with}~~C^{**}=C^{*}+2CC_{a}\frac{\lambda_{\max}}{\lambda_{\min}}.

(13)

Moreover, with probability at least
1-|{\mathcal{A}}^{c}|\exp(-cd)\cdot\sum_{l=t_{1}}^{t+1}{\mathbb{I}}\left\{\pi_{l}\neq 0\right\}
, we have for all
i\in{\mathcal{A}}^{c}
,

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{(t)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{*}\|^{2}\leq\frac{h^{2}}{4}.

(14)

Remark 5

(On Decaying Exploration and Its Relation to Exploration-Free Bandits)
.

A key implication of Theorem
4
(2) is that statistically optimal estimation rates for the optimal arms (those in
{\mathcal{A}}
) remain attainable even when the exploration rate
\pi_{t}
decays to zero. This provides rigorous theoretical justification for adaptive or gradually diminishing exploration schedules, and connects our framework to the emerging literature on
exploration-free
bandit algorithms. In particular,
4
introduce a class of “mostly exploration-free” algorithms that achieve optimal regret under a geometric condition on the covariate distribution, known as
covariate diversity
. This condition ensures that the randomness in the covariates supplies sufficient intrinsic exploration, thereby eliminating the need for explicit forced exploration. Our result offers a complementary and more general viewpoint: rather than relying on geometric assumptions about the covariates, we show that exploration becomes unnecessary once the estimation error satisfies
\max_{i}\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}^{(t)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\|\leq h/2
. In other words, explicit exploration is required only until the estimation error falls below a critical threshold; thereafter, the algorithm can operate under a near-greedy policy without compromising long-run performance. This insight highlights an algorithmic mechanism for substantially reducing or eliminating exploration, even in settings where geometric conditions such as covariate diversity fail or are difficult to verify.

Theorem
4
characterizes the estimation error rates in the presence of tail probabilities. Part (1) describes the convergence behavior when the exploration rate
\pi_{t}
